Yeah… I do. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly. I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body feels ready to be done for the day, but then as soon as I’m lying there and it’s quiet, my brain kind of turns on.

I start thinking through stuff — bills, whether I forgot to pay something, what my work schedule is going to look like, if I’m going to be too tired at work, things going on at home, whether everyone’s okay. It’s not even always one huge problem, it’s like a bunch of little things lining up and I just keep going over them. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about it, just sleep,” but that doesn’t really work.

So yeah, most nights it takes me a while to fall asleep. And even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel like I really settled down all the way. I wake up feeling like I never fully rested.

Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to call it “worrying” all the time because sometimes it feels like I’m just thinking things through, but honestly it’s most days. My mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next and doesn’t really stay quiet.

Like I’ll be thinking about money, then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us and whether that’s putting strain on everybody, then I’ll notice my wife seems stressed and I start worrying about her, and then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work and whether I messed something up at the register or if people think I’m not doing a good job. It’s like one concern hands off to the next one.

And it’s not always some huge crisis, you know? It’s more like my brain keeps making a list and then checking it over and over. Even if one thing is okay, there’s another thing right behind it. So… yeah, I’d say I worry a lot on most days.

My mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next and doesn’t really stay quiet.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us and whether that’s putting strain on everybody, then I’ll notice my wife seems stressed and I start worrying about her, and then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work and whether I messed something up at the register or if people think I’m not doing a good job. It’s like one concern hands off to the next one.\n\nAnd it’s not always some huge crisis, you know? It’s more like my brain keeps making a list and then checking it over and over. Even if one thing is okay, there’s another thing right behind it.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that’s the part that gets me. I can start off with one worry and tell myself, “Okay, just leave it alone,” or I’ll try to pray on it and let it be, because I know sitting there chewing on it isn’t helping anything. But once it starts, it’s like it gets momentum.

I’ll think, “What if I forgot something for work?” and then that turns into, “What if I get in trouble?” and then, “What if that messes up money?” and then I’m thinking about every bad way the whole day could go before it’s even happened. Even if I try to redirect myself, it keeps circling back, or it grabs onto the next thing.

So yeah, I can sometimes calm it down a little, but it’s hard to actually stop it once it gets going.

Yeah… definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been about a year and three months now, give or take. It really started after our older relative moved in with us. It’s like I’ll get what should be a full night’s sleep, but when I wake up, my body still feels heavy and my head feels kind of slow, like I’m already behind before the day even starts. \n\nI work as a cashier, and when I’m getting up for my shift, I just feel like I’m dragging myself through the morning. It’s not like I’m staying out all night or anything, so it’s been frustrating. I keep thinking I should feel rested, but I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I’m definitely frustrated and kind of worn down by feeling tired so much, and that can put me in a bad mood, but I haven’t really sat there thinking, like, “I’m depressed,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m just drained. Some days I’m quieter than usual or I don’t have much energy to deal with people, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling down. I guess I’d say not really depressed, more just… tired and annoyed that I can’t seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that you snore loudly when you sleep?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, nobody’s said anything to me about it, at least not like “you snore really loud” or anything. I don’t usually have someone watching me sleep, obviously, so I guess I can’t say for sure what I do the whole night, but it hasn’t been something anyone’s brought up.\n\nI’ve wondered about that kind of thing just because I wake up tired, but as far as I know, I’m not known for snoring or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually cold compared with other people?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually cold? I don’t think so. I mean, I get cold sometimes like anyone else, especially if I’m standing near the doors at work when people keep coming in and out, but I wouldn’t say I’m colder than other people in a way that’s stood out to me.\n\nLike, I haven’t been needing a jacket when everyone else is fine or anything like that. At home I’m pretty normal with the temperature, I think. So… no, I don’t think that’s really been a thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly. I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body feels ready to be done for the day, but then as soon as I’m lying there and it’s quiet, my brain kind of turns on.\n\nI start thinking through stuff — bills, whether I forgot to pay something, what my work schedule is going to look like, if I’m going to be too tired at work, things going on at home, whether everyone’s okay. It’s not even always one huge problem, it’s like a bunch of little things lining up and I just keep going over them. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about it, just sleep,” but that doesn’t really work.\n\nSo yeah, most nights it takes me a while to fall asleep. I wake up feeling like I never fully rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. My mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next and doesn’t really stay quiet.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us and whether that’s putting strain on everybody, then I’ll notice my wife seems stressed and I start worrying about her, and then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work and whether I messed something up at the register or if people think I’m not doing a good job. It’s like one concern hands off to the next one.\n\nAnd it’s not always some huge crisis, you know? It’s more like my brain keeps making a list and then checking it over and over. Even if one thing is okay, there’s another thing right behind it. So… yeah, I’d say I worry a lot on most days.\n\nDoctor: Is it hard for you to stop or control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that gets me. I can start off with one worry and tell myself, “Okay, just leave it alone,” or I’ll try to pray on it and let it be, because I know sitting there chewing on it isn’t helping anything. Even if I try to redirect myself, it keeps circling back, or it grabs onto the next thing.\n\nSo yeah, I can sometimes calm it down a little, but it’s hard to actually stop it once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of worrying been going on for 6 months or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been about a year and three months now, give or take. It really started after our older relative moved in with us. I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body feels ready to be done for the day, but then as soon as I’m lying there and it’s quiet, my brain kind of turns on.\n\nI start thinking through stuff — bills, whether I forgot to pay something, what my work schedule is going to look like, if I’m going to be too tired at work, things going on at home, whether everyone’s okay. It’s not even always one huge problem, it’s like a bunch of little things lining up and I just keep going over them. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about it, just sleep,” but that doesn’t really work.\n\nSo yeah, most nights it takes me a while to fall asleep. And even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel like I really settled down all the way. I wake up feeling like I never fully rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to call it “worrying” all the time because sometimes it feels like I’m just thinking things through, but honestly it’s most days. My mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next and doesn’t really stay quiet.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us and whether that’s putting strain on everybody, then I’ll notice my wife seems stressed and I start worrying about her, and then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work and whether I messed something up at the register or if people think I’m not doing a good job. It’s like one concern hands off to the next one.\n\nAnd it’s not always some huge crisis, you know? It’s more like my brain keeps making a list and then checking it over and over. Even if one thing is okay, there’s another thing right behind it. So… yeah, I’d say I worry a lot on most days.\n\nDoctor: Is it hard for you to stop or control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that gets me. I can start off with one worry and tell myself, “Okay, just leave it alone,” or I’ll try to pray on it and let it be, because I know sitting there chewing on it isn’t helping anything. But once it starts, it’s like it gets momentum.\n\nI’ll think, “What if I forgot something for work?” and then that turns into, “What if I get in trouble?” and then, “What if that messes up money?” and then I’m thinking about every bad way the whole day could go before it’s even happened. Even if I try to redirect myself, it keeps circling back, or it grabs onto the next thing.\n\nSo yeah, I can sometimes calm it down a little, but it’s hard to actually stop it once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of worrying been going on for 6 months or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been about a year and three months now, give or take. It really started after our older relative moved in with us. At first I thought it was just an adjustment period, like everybody getting used to a different routine in the house, but it just kind of kept going.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not something that only started recently. It’s been over a year at this point.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I’d call it “keyed up” exactly. I mean, I’m not bouncing off the walls or anything like that. But I do have times where I feel like I’m kind of braced for something, if that makes sense… like I’m waiting for the next thing to need my attention.\n\nIt’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is. Sometimes I only notice it after I’ve already been sitting there tense for a while. But I guess I wouldn’t say I feel relaxed as much as I probably should. I’m just not sure if that’s the same as being on edge.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I walk around thinking, “My muscles are tense,” if that makes sense. I’m not someone who pays a lot of attention to my body unless something is really bothering me.

I don’t think so. I mean, I get cold sometimes like anyone else, especially if I’m standing near the doors at work when people keep coming in and out, but I wouldn’t say I’m colder than other people in a way that’s stood out to me.\n\nLike, I haven’t been needing a jacket when everyone else is fine or anything like that. At home I’m pretty normal with the temperature, I think. So… no, I don’t think that’s really been a thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly. I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body feels ready to be done for the day, but then as soon as I’m lying there and it’s quiet, my brain kind of turns on.\n\nI start thinking through stuff — bills, whether I forgot to pay something, what my work schedule is going to look like, if I’m going to be too tired at work, things going on at home, whether everyone’s okay. It’s not even always one huge problem, it’s like a bunch of little things lining up and I just keep going over them. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about it, just sleep,” but that doesn’t really work.\n\nSo yeah, most nights it takes me a while to fall asleep. My mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next and doesn’t really stay quiet.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us and whether that’s putting strain on everybody, then I’ll notice my wife seems stressed and I start worrying about her, and then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work and whether I messed something up at the register or if people think I’m not doing a good job. Yeah, that does happen. Not all day every day, but enough that I notice it, especially at work. Like, I’ll be at the register and I’m literally looking right at the screen, scanning things, taking cash, all of that, and then I realize my mind has kind of gone somewhere else — usually thinking about stuff at home or bills, what needs to be paid, whether something’s going to come up.

And even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel like I really settled down all the way. I wake up feeling like I never fully rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to call it “worrying” all the time because sometimes it feels like I’m just thinking things through, but honestly it’s most days. My mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next and doesn’t really stay quiet.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us and whether that’s putting strain on everybody, then I’ll notice my wife seems stressed and I start worrying about her, and then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work and whether I messed something up at the register or if people think I’m not doing a good job. It’s like one concern hands off to the next one.\n\nAnd it’s not always some huge crisis, you know? It’s more like my brain keeps making a list and then checking it over and over. But once it starts, it’s like it gets momentum.\n\nI’ll think, “What if I forgot something for work?” and then that turns into, “What if I get in trouble?” and then, “What if that messes up money?” and then I’m thinking about every bad way the whole day could go before it’s even happened. Even if I try to redirect myself, it keeps circling back, or it grabs onto the next thing.\n\nSo yeah, I can sometimes calm it down a little, but it’s hard to actually stop it once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of worrying been going on for 6 months or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been about a year and three months now, give or take. It really started after our older relative moved in with us. At first I thought it was just an adjustment period, like everybody getting used to a different routine in the house, but it just kind of kept going.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not something that only started recently. It’s been over a year at this point.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I’d call it “keyed up” exactly. I mean, I’m not bouncing off the walls or anything like that. But I do have times where I feel like I’m kind of braced for something, if that makes sense… like I’m waiting for the next thing to need my attention.\n\nIt’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is. Sometimes I only notice it after I’ve already been sitting there tense for a while. But I guess I wouldn’t say I feel relaxed as much as I probably should. I’m just not sure if that’s the same as being on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I walk around thinking, “My muscles are tense,” if that makes sense. I’m not someone who pays a lot of attention to my body unless something is really bothering me.\n\nSometimes I’ll notice my shoulders are kind of up, or my jaw feels a little tight, but I don’t know if that’s a regular thing or just, you know, normal from working or standing around. Like at the register, I’m on my feet and doing the same motions, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s stress and what’s just being tired from the day.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I often notice it, no. Maybe here and there, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is preoccupied with worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen. Not all day every day, but enough that I notice it, especially at work. Like, I’ll be at the register and I’m literally looking right at the screen, scanning things, taking cash, all of that, and then I realize my mind has kind of gone somewhere else — usually thinking about stuff at home or bills, what needs to be paid, whether something’s going to come up.\n\nAnd then I lose track of where I was. Sometimes it’s small, like I have to pause and double-check the total or recount the drawer because I don’t trust that I followed it right the first time. Or I’ll catch a small mistake and think, “Okay, I need to focus,” but it’s frustrating because I was trying to focus. It’s like the worry is running in the background and it pulls me away even when I’m doing something pretty straightforward.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’ve been more irritable than usual. I mean, everybody gets annoyed sometimes, especially when work is busy or people are impatient in line, but I haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people or feeling angry all the time.\n\nMaybe I get a little short in my head, if that makes sense — like I’ll feel frustrated faster than I’d like — but I don’t know if that’s actually more than usual or just normal stress. I guess I’d say not really depressed, more just… tired and annoyed that I can’t seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that you snore loudly when you sleep?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, nobody’s said anything to me about it, at least not like “you snore really loud” or anything. I don’t usually have someone watching me sleep, obviously, so I guess I can’t say for sure what I do the whole night, but it hasn’t been something anyone’s brought up.\n\nI’ve wondered about that kind of thing just because I wake up tired, but as far as I know, I’m not known for snoring or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually cold compared with other people?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually cold? I don’t think so. I mean, I get cold sometimes like anyone else, especially if I’m standing near the doors at work when people keep coming in and out, but I wouldn’t say I’m colder than other people in a way that’s stood out to me.\n\nLike, I haven’t been needing a jacket when everyone else is fine or anything like that. At home I’m pretty normal with the temperature, I think. So… no, I don’t think that’s really been a thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly. I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body feels ready to be done for the day, but then as soon as I’m lying there and it’s quiet, my brain kind of turns on.\n\nI start thinking through stuff — bills, whether I forgot to pay something, what my work schedule is going to look like, if I’m going to be too tired at work, things going on at home, whether everyone’s okay. It’s not even always one huge problem, it’s like a bunch of little things lining up and I just keep going over them. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about it, just sleep,” but that doesn’t really work.\n\nSo yeah, most nights it takes me a while to fall asleep. And even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel like I really settled down all the way. I wake up feeling like I never fully rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to call it “worrying” all the time because sometimes it feels like I’m just thinking things through, but honestly it’s most days. My mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next and doesn’t really stay quiet.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us and whether that’s putting strain on everybody, then I’ll notice my wife seems stressed and I start worrying about her, and then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work and whether I messed something up at the register or if people think I’m not doing a good job. It’s like one concern hands off to the next one.\n\nAnd it’s not always some huge crisis, you know? It’s more like my brain keeps making a list and then checking it over and over. Even if one thing is okay, there’s another thing right behind it. So… yeah, I’d say I worry a lot on most days.\n\nDoctor: Is it hard for you to stop or control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that gets me. I can start off with one worry and tell myself, “Okay, just leave it alone,” or I’ll try to pray on it and let it be, because I know sitting there chewing on it isn’t helping anything. But once it starts, it’s like it gets momentum.\n\nI’ll think, “What if I forgot something for work?” and then that turns into, “What if I get in trouble?” and then, “What if that messes up money?” and then I’m thinking about every bad way the whole day could go before it’s even happened. Even if I try to redirect myself, it keeps circling back, or it grabs onto the next thing.\n\nSo yeah, I can sometimes calm it down a little, but it’s hard to actually stop it once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of worrying been going on for 6 months or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been about a year and three months now, give or take. It really started after our older relative moved in with us. At first I thought it was just an adjustment period, like everybody getting used to a different routine in the house, but it just kind of kept going.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not something that only started recently. It’s been over a year at this point.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I’d call it “keyed up” exactly. I mean, I’m not bouncing off the walls or anything like that. But I do have times where I feel like I’m kind of braced for something, if that makes sense… like I’m waiting for the next thing to need my attention.\n\nIt’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is. Sometimes I only notice it after I’ve already been sitting there tense for a while. But I guess I wouldn’t say I feel relaxed as much as I probably should. I’m just not sure if that’s the same as being on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I walk around thinking, “My muscles are tense,” if that makes sense. I’m not someone who pays a lot of attention to my body unless something is really bothering me.\n\nSometimes I’ll notice my shoulders are kind of up, or my jaw feels a little tight, but I don’t know if that’s a regular thing or just, you know, normal from working or standing around. Like at the register, I’m on my feet and doing the same motions, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s stress and what’s just being tired from the day.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I often notice it, no. Maybe here and there, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is preoccupied with worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen. Not all day every day, but enough that I notice it, especially at work. Like, I’ll be at the register and I’m literally looking right at the screen, scanning things, taking cash, all of that, and then I realize my mind has kind of gone somewhere else — usually thinking about stuff at home or bills, what needs to be paid, whether something’s going to come up.\n\nAnd then I lose track of where I was. Sometimes it’s small, like I have to pause and double-check the total or recount the drawer because I don’t trust that I followed it right the first time. Or I’ll catch a small mistake and think, “Okay, I need to focus,” but it’s frustrating because I was trying to focus. It’s like the worry is running in the background and it pulls me away even when I’m doing something pretty straightforward.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’ve been more irritable than usual. I mean, everybody gets annoyed sometimes, especially when work is busy or people are impatient in line, but I haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people or feeling angry all the time.\n\nMaybe I get a little short in my head, if that makes sense — like I’ll feel frustrated faster than I’d like — but I don’t know if that’s actually more than usual or just normal stress. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m being especially irritable or anything. So… I’m not sure. It’s not one of the main things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I feel worn out almost every day, and not just like, “Oh, I had a long shift” tired. It’s more like I wake up already dragging, even if I got what should be a full night’s sleep.\n\nI’ll get up for my cashier shift and my body feels heavy, and my head feels kind of slow, like I’m starting the day behind before I’ve even done anything. And then standing at the register all day, talking to people, keeping track of everything — it feels like it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I do feel easily fatigued during the day. It’s pretty frustrating because I keep thinking sleep should fix it, but a lot of mornings it just doesn’t feel like it does.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

It’s pretty frustrating because I keep thinking sleep should fix it, but a lot of mornings it just doesn’t feel like it does.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo your worries happen during the day too, or mostly only when you’re trying to fall asleep at night?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / cashier / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"It feels like my mind is always jumping from one thing to the next—money, the relative staying with us, my wife being stressed, church duties, and whether I'm messing up at work.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It happens during the day too, definitely. It’s not just when I’m lying there trying to fall asleep, though that can be when it gets louder because there’s nothing else going on.

But during the day, my mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Like I’ll be at work and start thinking about money — what’s due, what we can cover, what we can’t — and then it shifts to the relative staying with us, and whether that’s putting more pressure on things at home. Then I’ll think about my wife being stressed, and if I’m doing enough to help her, and then somehow it jumps to church stuff I said I’d help with, or whether I forgot something.

And at work it turns into, like, “Am I messing this up? Did I count that right? Did I miss something?” So yeah, it’s kind of all through the day. It’s like my brain doesn’t stay on one worry — it just keeps moving down the list.

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. It’s like I’ll get what should be a full night’s sleep, but when I wake up, my body still feels heavy and my head feels kind of slow, like I’m already behind before the day even starts. \n\nI work as a cashier, and when I’m getting up for my shift, I just feel like I’m dragging myself through the morning. It’s not like I’m staying out all night or anything, so it’s been frustrating. I keep thinking I should feel rested, but I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I’m definitely frustrated and kind of worn down by feeling tired so much, and that can put me in a bad mood, but I haven’t really sat there thinking, like, “I’m depressed,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m just drained. Some days I’m quieter than usual or I don’t have much energy to deal with people, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling down. I guess I’d say not really depressed, more just… tired and annoyed that I can’t seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that you snore loudly when you sleep?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, nobody’s said anything to me about it, at least not like “you snore really loud” or anything. I don’t usually have someone watching me sleep, obviously, so I guess I can’t say for sure what I do the whole night, but it hasn’t been something anyone’s brought up.\n\nI’ve wondered about that kind of thing just because I wake up tired, but as far as I know, I’m not known for snoring or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually cold compared with other people?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually cold? I don’t think so. I mean, I get cold sometimes like anyone else, especially if I’m standing near the doors at work when people keep coming in and out, but I wouldn’t say I’m colder than other people in a way that’s stood out to me.\n\nLike, I haven’t been needing a jacket when everyone else is fine or anything like that. At home I’m pretty normal with the temperature, I think. So… no, I don’t think that’s really been a thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly. I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body feels ready to be done for the day, but then as soon as I’m lying there and it’s quiet, my brain kind of turns on.\n\nI start thinking through stuff — bills, whether I forgot to pay something, what my work schedule is going to look like, if I’m going to be too tired at work, things going on at home, whether everyone’s okay. It’s not even always one huge problem, it’s like a bunch of little things lining up and I just keep going over them. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about it, just sleep,” but that doesn’t really work.\n\nSo yeah, most nights it takes me a while to fall asleep. And even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel like I really settled down all the way. I wake up feeling like I never fully rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to call it “worrying” all the time because sometimes it feels like I’m just thinking things through, but honestly it’s most days. My mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next and doesn’t really stay quiet.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us and whether that’s putting strain on everybody, then I’ll notice my wife seems stressed and I start worrying about her, and then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work and whether I messed something up at the register or if people think I’m not doing a good job. It’s like one concern hands off to the next one.\n\nAnd it’s not always some huge crisis, you know? It’s more like my brain keeps making a list and then checking it over and over. Even if one thing is okay, there’s another thing right behind it. So… yeah, I’d say I worry a lot on most days.\n\nDoctor: Is it hard for you to stop or control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that gets me. I can start off with one worry and tell myself, “Okay, just leave it alone,” or I’ll try to pray on it and let it be, because I know sitting there chewing on it isn’t helping anything. But once it starts, it’s like it gets momentum.\n\nI’ll think, “What if I forgot something for work?” and then that turns into, “What if I get in trouble?” and then, “What if that messes up money?” and then I’m thinking about every bad way the whole day could go before it’s even happened. Even if I try to redirect myself, it keeps circling back, or it grabs onto the next thing.\n\nSo yeah, I can sometimes calm it down a little, but it’s hard to actually stop it once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of worrying been going on for 6 months or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been about a year and three months now, give or take. It really started after our older relative moved in with us. At first I thought it was just an adjustment period, like everybody getting used to a different routine in the house, but it just kind of kept going.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not something that only started recently. It’s been over a year at this point.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I’d call it “keyed up” exactly. I mean, I’m not bouncing off the walls or anything like that. But I do have times where I feel like I’m kind of braced for something, if that makes sense… like I’m waiting for the next thing to need my attention.\n\nIt’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is. Sometimes I only notice it after I’ve already been sitting there tense for a while. But I guess I wouldn’t say I feel relaxed as much as I probably should. I’m just not sure if that’s the same as being on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I walk around thinking, “My muscles are tense,” if that makes sense. I’m not someone who pays a lot of attention to my body unless something is really bothering me.\n\nSometimes I’ll notice my shoulders are kind of up, or my jaw feels a little tight, but I don’t know if that’s a regular thing or just, you know, normal from working or standing around. Like at the register, I’m on my feet and doing the same motions, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s stress and what’s just being tired from the day.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I often notice it, no. Maybe here and there, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is preoccupied with worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen. Not all day every day, but enough that I notice it, especially at work. Like, I’ll be at the register and I’m literally looking right at the screen, scanning things, taking cash, all of that, and then I realize my mind has kind of gone somewhere else — usually thinking about stuff at home or bills, what needs to be paid, whether something’s going to come up.\n\nAnd then I lose track of where I was. Sometimes it’s small, like I have to pause and double-check the total or recount the drawer because I don’t trust that I followed it right the first time. Or I’ll catch a small mistake and think, “Okay, I need to focus,” but it’s frustrating because I was trying to focus. It’s like the worry is running in the background and it pulls me away even when I’m doing something pretty straightforward.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’ve been more irritable than usual. I mean, everybody gets annoyed sometimes, especially when work is busy or people are impatient in line, but I haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people or feeling angry all the time.\n\nMaybe I get a little short in my head, if that makes sense — like I’ll feel frustrated faster than I’d like — but I don’t know if that’s actually more than usual or just normal stress. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m being especially irritable or anything. So… I’m not sure. track because I'm thinking about home or bills, and that's when I end up having to recount the drawer or catch a small mistake.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n can keep up with everything.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and three months, since our older relative moved in with us.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / cashier / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S029\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’ve been more irritable than usual. I mean, everybody gets annoyed sometimes, especially when work is busy or people are impatient in line, but I haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people or feeling angry all the time.\n\nMaybe I get a little short in my head, if that makes sense — like I’ll feel frustrated faster than I’d like — but I don’t know if that’s actually more than usual or just normal stress. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m being especially irritable or anything. So… I’m not sure. It’s not one of the main things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I feel worn out almost every day, and not just like, “Oh, I had a long shift” tired. It’s more like I wake up already dragging, even if I got what should be a full night’s sleep.\n\nI’ll get up for my cashier shift and my body feels heavy, and my head feels kind of slow, like I’m starting the day behind before I’ve even done anything. And then standing at the register all day, talking to people, keeping track of everything — it feels like it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I do feel easily fatigued during the day. It’s pretty frustrating because I keep thinking sleep should fix it, but a lot of mornings it just doesn’t feel like it does.\n\nDoctor: Do your worries happen during the day too, or mostly only when you’re trying to fall asleep at night?\n\nPatient: It happens during the day too, definitely. It’s not just when I’m lying there trying to fall asleep, though that can be when it gets louder because there’s nothing else going on.\n\nBut during the day, my mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Like I’ll be at work and start thinking about money — what’s due, what we can cover, what we can’t — and then it shifts to the relative staying with us, and whether that’s putting more pressure on things at home. Then I’ll think about my wife being stressed, and if I’m doing enough to help her, and then somehow it jumps to church stuff I said I’d help with, or whether I forgot something.\n\nAnd at work it turns into, like, “Am I messing this up? Did I count that right? Did I miss something?” So yeah, it’s kind of all through the day. It’s like my brain doesn’t stay on one worry — it just keeps moving down the list.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel restless?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Restless… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s pacing around all the time or unable to sit still, if that’s what you mean.

Sometimes I do feel kind of unsettled, like I can’t fully relax, but I don’t know if I’d call it “restless” exactly. At work I’m standing and moving anyway, so it’s hard to tell. And at home, I might shift around or feel like I should be doing something, but I haven’t really paid attention to it as a separate thing.

So… maybe a little? But I wouldn’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed or that stands out to me.

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. It’s like I’ll get what should be a full night’s sleep, but when I wake up, my body still feels heavy and my head feels kind of slow, like I’m already behind before the day even starts. \n\nI work as a cashier, and when I’m getting up for my shift, I just feel like I’m dragging myself through the morning. It’s not like I’m staying out all night or anything, so it’s been frustrating. I keep thinking I should feel rested, but I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I’m definitely frustrated and kind of worn down by feeling tired so much, and that can put me in a bad mood, but I haven’t really sat there thinking, like, “I’m depressed,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m just drained. Some days I’m quieter than usual or I don’t have much energy to deal with people, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling down. I guess I’d say not really depressed, more just… tired and annoyed that I can’t seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that you snore loudly when you sleep?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, nobody’s said anything to me about it, at least not like “you snore really loud” or anything. I don’t usually have someone watching me sleep, obviously, so I guess I can’t say for sure what I do the whole night, but it hasn’t been something anyone’s brought up.\n\nI’ve wondered about that kind of thing just because I wake up tired, but as far as I know, I’m not known for snoring or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually cold compared with other people?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually cold? I don’t think so. I mean, I get cold sometimes like anyone else, especially if I’m standing near the doors at work when people keep coming in and out, but I wouldn’t say I’m colder than other people in a way that’s stood out to me.\n\nLike, I haven’t been needing a jacket when everyone else is fine or anything like that. At home I’m pretty normal with the temperature, I think. So… no, I don’t think that’s really been a thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly. I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body feels ready to be done for the day, but then as soon as I’m lying there and it’s quiet, my brain kind of turns on.\n\nI start thinking through stuff — bills, whether I forgot to pay something, what my work schedule is going to look like, if I’m going to be too tired at work, things going on at home, whether everyone’s okay. It’s not even always one huge problem, it’s like a bunch of little things lining up and I just keep going over them. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about it, just sleep,” but that doesn’t really work.\n\nSo yeah, most nights it takes me a while to fall asleep. And even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel like I really settled down all the way. I wake up feeling like I never fully rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to call it “worrying” all the time because sometimes it feels like I’m just thinking things through, but honestly it’s most days. My mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next and doesn’t really stay quiet.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us and whether that’s putting strain on everybody, then I’ll notice my wife seems stressed and I start worrying about her, and then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work and whether I messed something up at the register or if people think I’m not doing a good job. It’s like one concern hands off to the next one.\n\nAnd it’s not always some huge crisis, you know? It’s more like my brain keeps making a list and then checking it over and over. Even if one thing is okay, there’s another thing right behind it. So… yeah, I’d say I worry a lot on most days.\n\nDoctor: Is it hard for you to stop or control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that gets me. I can start off with one worry and tell myself, “Okay, just leave it alone,” or I’ll try to pray on it and let it be, because I know sitting there chewing on it isn’t helping anything. But once it starts, it’s like it gets momentum.\n\nI’ll think, “What if I forgot something for work?” and then that turns into, “What if I get in trouble?” and then, “What if that messes up money?” and then I’m thinking about every bad way the whole day could go before it’s even happened. Even if I try to redirect myself, it keeps circling back, or it grabs onto the next thing.\n\nSo yeah, I can sometimes calm it down a little, but it’s hard to actually stop it once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of worrying been going on for 6 months or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been about a year and three months now, give or take. It really started after our older relative moved in with us. At first I thought it was just an adjustment period, like everybody getting used to a different routine in the house, but it just kind of kept going.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not something that only started recently. It’s been over a year at this point.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I’d call it “keyed up” exactly. I mean, I’m not bouncing off the walls or anything like that. But I do have times where I feel like I’m kind of braced for something, if that makes sense… like I’m waiting for the next thing to need my attention.\n\nIt’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is. Sometimes I only notice it after I’ve already been sitting there tense for a while. But I guess I wouldn’t say I feel relaxed as much as I probably should. I’m just not sure if that’s the same as being on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I walk around thinking, “My muscles are tense,” if that makes sense. I’m not someone who pays a lot of attention to my body unless something is really bothering me.\n\nSometimes I’ll notice my shoulders are kind of up, or my jaw feels a little tight, but I don’t know if that’s a regular thing or just, you know, normal from working or standing around. Like at the register, I’m on my feet and doing the same motions, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s stress and what’s just being tired from the day.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I often notice it, no. Maybe here and there, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is preoccupied with worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen. Not all day every day, but enough that I notice it, especially at work. Like, I’ll be at the register and I’m literally looking right at the screen, scanning things, taking cash, all of that, and then I realize my mind has kind of gone somewhere else — usually thinking about stuff at home or bills, what needs to be paid, whether something’s going to come up.\n\nAnd then I lose track of where I was. Sometimes it’s small, like I have to pause and double-check the total or recount the drawer because I don’t trust that I followed it right the first time. Or I’ll catch a small mistake and think, “Okay, I need to focus,” but it’s frustrating because I was trying to focus. It’s like the worry is running in the background and it pulls me away even when I’m doing something pretty straightforward.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’ve been more irritable than usual. I mean, everybody gets annoyed sometimes, especially when work is busy or people are impatient in line, but I haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people or feeling angry all the time.\n\nMaybe I get a little short in my head, if that makes sense — like I’ll feel frustrated faster than I’d like — but I don’t know if that’s actually more than usual or just normal stress. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m being especially irritable or anything. So… I’m not sure. It’s not one of the main things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I’m not someone who pays a lot of attention to my body unless something is really bothering me.\n\nSometimes I’ll notice my shoulders are kind of up, or my jaw feels a little tight, but I don’t know if that’s a regular thing or just, you know, normal from working or standing around. Like at the register, I’m on my feet and doing the same motions, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s stress and what’s just being tired from the day.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I often notice it, no. Maybe here and there, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is preoccupied with worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen. Not all day every day, but enough that I notice it, especially at work. Like, I’ll be at the register and I’m literally looking right at the screen, scanning things, taking cash, all of that, and then I realize my mind has kind of gone somewhere else — usually thinking about stuff at home or bills, what needs to be paid, whether something’s going to come up.\n\nAnd then I lose track of where I was. Sometimes it’s small, like I have to pause and double-check the total or recount the drawer because I don’t trust that I followed it right the first time. Or I’ll catch a small mistake and think, “Okay, I need to focus,” but it’s frustrating because I was trying to focus. It’s like the worry is running in the background and it pulls me away even when I’m doing something pretty straightforward.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’ve been more irritable than usual. I mean, everybody gets annoyed sometimes, especially when work is busy or people are impatient in line, but I haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people or feeling angry all the time.\n\nMaybe I get a little short in my head, if that makes sense — like I’ll feel frustrated faster than I’d like — but I don’t know if that’s actually more than usual or just normal stress. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m being especially irritable or anything. So… I’m not sure. It’s not one of the main things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I feel worn out almost every day, and not just like, “Oh, I had a long shift” tired. It’s more like I wake up already dragging, even if I got what should be a full night’s sleep.\n\nI’ll get up for my cashier shift and my body feels heavy, and my head feels kind of slow, like I’m starting the day behind before I’ve even done anything. And then standing at the register all day, talking to people, keeping track of everything — it feels like it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I do feel easily fatigued during the day. It’s pretty frustrating because I keep thinking sleep should fix it, but a lot of mornings it just doesn’t feel like it does.\n\nDoctor: Do your worries happen during the day too, or mostly only when you’re trying to fall asleep at night?\n\nPatient: It happens during the day too, definitely. It’s not just when I’m lying there trying to fall asleep, though that can be when it gets louder because there’s nothing else going on.\n\nBut during the day, my mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Like I’ll be at work and start thinking about money — what’s due, what we can cover, what we can’t — and then it shifts to the relative staying with us, and whether that’s putting more pressure on things at home. Then I’ll think about my wife being stressed, and if I’m doing enough to help her, and then somehow it jumps to church stuff I said I’d help with, or whether I forgot something.\n\nAnd at work it turns into, like, “Am I messing this up? Did I count that right? Did I miss something?” So yeah, it’s kind of all through the day. It’s like my brain doesn’t stay on one worry — it just keeps moving down the list.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. At work I’m standing and moving anyway, so it’s hard to tell. Yeah… I think they have, though I still get through the day, so sometimes I tell myself it’s not that bad. But it is affecting things.

At work, for example, I’ve been double-checking my cash drawer a lot more than I used to because I’ve made these small mistakes, or I worry that I did. So I’ll count things again, or go back over it in my head, and it slows me down. I don’t think I’m falling apart at work or anything, but it makes the shift feel harder, and I feel less confident doing something I’ve done for a long time.

And outside of work, I’ve cut back on volunteering at the food pantry. I used to help more regularly, and I still care about it, but lately I just feel like I can’t keep up with everything. It’s like adding one more responsibility makes me feel overloaded, even if it’s something good. Then I feel guilty for stepping back, but also relieved because I don’t know if I could handle it on top of everything else.

So yes, day to day I’m functioning, but it’s taking more effort, and I’ve had to pull back from some things.

Yeah, it’s definitely not just one thing. If it were only one problem, maybe it would feel easier to pin down, but it’s more like my mind keeps hopping around.

I’ll start out thinking about money — like what bills are coming up, whether we’re stretched too thin — and then before I’ve even settled that in my head, I’m thinking about the relative staying with us and how that affects the house. Then I start worrying about my wife being stressed and whether I’m doing enough or making it worse somehow. And then it’ll jump to church responsibilities, like something I said I’d help with, or whether I forgot to follow through.

And then work gets mixed in too. I’ll be thinking, “Did I mess up? Did I count something wrong? Am I going to make another mistake?” So it’s not really one specific problem. It feels like there’s a whole list, and my brain just keeps moving down it, even when I’m trying to focus on whatever’s actually in front of me.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, G47.00, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. It’s like I’ll get what should be a full night’s sleep, but when I wake up, my body still feels heavy and my head feels kind of slow, like I’m already behind before the day even starts. \n\nI work as a cashier, and when I’m getting up for my shift, I just feel like I’m dragging myself through the morning. It’s not like I’m staying out all night or anything, so it’s been frustrating. I keep thinking I should feel rested, but I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I’m definitely frustrated and kind of worn down by feeling tired so much, and that can put me in a bad mood, but I haven’t really sat there thinking, like, “I’m depressed,” if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… I’m just drained. Some days I’m quieter than usual or I don’t have much energy to deal with people, but I’m not sure if that’s the same thing as feeling down. I guess I’d say not really depressed, more just… tired and annoyed that I can’t seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that you snore loudly when you sleep?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, nobody’s said anything to me about it, at least not like “you snore really loud” or anything. I don’t usually have someone watching me sleep, obviously, so I guess I can’t say for sure what I do the whole night, but it hasn’t been something anyone’s brought up.\n\nI’ve wondered about that kind of thing just because I wake up tired, but as far as I know, I’m not known for snoring or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually cold compared with other people?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually cold? I don’t think so. I mean, I get cold sometimes like anyone else, especially if I’m standing near the doors at work when people keep coming in and out, but I wouldn’t say I’m colder than other people in a way that’s stood out to me.\n\nLike, I haven’t been needing a jacket when everyone else is fine or anything like that. At home I’m pretty normal with the temperature, I think. So… no, I don’t think that’s really been a thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly. I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body feels ready to be done for the day, but then as soon as I’m lying there and it’s quiet, my brain kind of turns on.\n\nI start thinking through stuff — bills, whether I forgot to pay something, what my work schedule is going to look like, if I’m going to be too tired at work, things going on at home, whether everyone’s okay. It’s not even always one huge problem, it’s like a bunch of little things lining up and I just keep going over them. And I’ll tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about it, just sleep,” but that doesn’t really work.\n\nSo yeah, most nights it takes me a while to fall asleep. And even when I do finally sleep, it doesn’t feel like I really settled down all the way. I wake up feeling like I never fully rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I try not to call it “worrying” all the time because sometimes it feels like I’m just thinking things through, but honestly it’s most days. My mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next and doesn’t really stay quiet.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us and whether that’s putting strain on everybody, then I’ll notice my wife seems stressed and I start worrying about her, and then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, and then suddenly I’m thinking about work and whether I messed something up at the register or if people think I’m not doing a good job. It’s like one concern hands off to the next one.\n\nAnd it’s not always some huge crisis, you know? It’s more like my brain keeps making a list and then checking it over and over. Even if one thing is okay, there’s another thing right behind it. So… yeah, I’d say I worry a lot on most days.\n\nDoctor: Is it hard for you to stop or control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that gets me. I can start off with one worry and tell myself, “Okay, just leave it alone,” or I’ll try to pray on it and let it be, because I know sitting there chewing on it isn’t helping anything. But once it starts, it’s like it gets momentum.\n\nI’ll think, “What if I forgot something for work?” and then that turns into, “What if I get in trouble?” and then, “What if that messes up money?” and then I’m thinking about every bad way the whole day could go before it’s even happened. Even if I try to redirect myself, it keeps circling back, or it grabs onto the next thing.\n\nSo yeah, I can sometimes calm it down a little, but it’s hard to actually stop it once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of worrying been going on for 6 months or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been about a year and three months now, give or take. It really started after our older relative moved in with us. At first I thought it was just an adjustment period, like everybody getting used to a different routine in the house, but it just kind of kept going.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not something that only started recently. It’s been over a year at this point.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I’d call it “keyed up” exactly. I mean, I’m not bouncing off the walls or anything like that. But I do have times where I feel like I’m kind of braced for something, if that makes sense… like I’m waiting for the next thing to need my attention.\n\nIt’s not constant, or at least I don’t think it is. Sometimes I only notice it after I’ve already been sitting there tense for a while. But I guess I wouldn’t say I feel relaxed as much as I probably should. I’m just not sure if that’s the same as being on edge.\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense or tight?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I walk around thinking, “My muscles are tense,” if that makes sense. I’m not someone who pays a lot of attention to my body unless something is really bothering me.\n\nSometimes I’ll notice my shoulders are kind of up, or my jaw feels a little tight, but I don’t know if that’s a regular thing or just, you know, normal from working or standing around. Like at the register, I’m on my feet and doing the same motions, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s stress and what’s just being tired from the day.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I often notice it, no. Maybe here and there, but it’s not something I’ve really been tracking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is preoccupied with worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen. Not all day every day, but enough that I notice it, especially at work. Like, I’ll be at the register and I’m literally looking right at the screen, scanning things, taking cash, all of that, and then I realize my mind has kind of gone somewhere else — usually thinking about stuff at home or bills, what needs to be paid, whether something’s going to come up.\n\nAnd then I lose track of where I was. Sometimes it’s small, like I have to pause and double-check the total or recount the drawer because I don’t trust that I followed it right the first time. Or I’ll catch a small mistake and think, “Okay, I need to focus,” but it’s frustrating because I was trying to focus. It’s like the worry is running in the background and it pulls me away even when I’m doing something pretty straightforward.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’ve been more irritable than usual. I mean, everybody gets annoyed sometimes, especially when work is busy or people are impatient in line, but I haven’t really noticed myself snapping at people or feeling angry all the time.\n\nMaybe I get a little short in my head, if that makes sense — like I’ll feel frustrated faster than I’d like — but I don’t know if that’s actually more than usual or just normal stress. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m being especially irritable or anything. So… I’m not sure. It’s not one of the main things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually one of the main reasons I came in. I feel worn out almost every day, and not just like, “Oh, I had a long shift” tired. It’s more like I wake up already dragging, even if I got what should be a full night’s sleep.\n\nI’ll get up for my cashier shift and my body feels heavy, and my head feels kind of slow, like I’m starting the day behind before I’ve even done anything. And then standing at the register all day, talking to people, keeping track of everything — it feels like it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nSo yes, I’d say I do feel easily fatigued during the day. It’s pretty frustrating because I keep thinking sleep should fix it, but a lot of mornings it just doesn’t feel like it does.\n\nDoctor: Do your worries happen during the day too, or mostly only when you’re trying to fall asleep at night?\n\nPatient: It happens during the day too, definitely. It’s not just when I’m lying there trying to fall asleep, though that can be when it gets louder because there’s nothing else going on.\n\nBut during the day, my mind kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Like I’ll be at work and start thinking about money — what’s due, what we can cover, what we can’t — and then it shifts to the relative staying with us, and whether that’s putting more pressure on things at home. Then I’ll think about my wife being stressed, and if I’m doing enough to help her, and then somehow it jumps to church stuff I said I’d help with, or whether I forgot something.\n\nAnd at work it turns into, like, “Am I messing this up? Did I count that right? Did I miss something?” So yeah, it’s kind of all through the day. It’s like my brain doesn’t stay on one worry — it just keeps moving down the list.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s pacing around all the time or unable to sit still, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I do feel kind of unsettled, like I can’t fully relax, but I don’t know if I’d call it “restless” exactly. At work I’m standing and moving anyway, so it’s hard to tell. And at home, I might shift around or feel like I should be doing something, but I haven’t really paid attention to it as a separate thing.\n\nSo… maybe a little? But I wouldn’t say it’s something I’ve clearly noticed or that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been making it hard for you to function day to day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they have, though I still get through the day, so sometimes I tell myself it’s not that bad. But it is affecting things.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve been double-checking my cash drawer a lot more than I used to because I’ve made these small mistakes, or I worry that I did. So I’ll count things again, or go back over it in my head, and it slows me down. I don’t think I’m falling apart at work or anything, but it makes the shift feel harder, and I feel less confident doing something I’ve done for a long time.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve cut back on volunteering at the food pantry. I used to help more regularly, and I still care about it, but lately I just feel like I can’t keep up with everything. It’s like adding one more responsibility makes me feel overloaded, even if it’s something good. Then I feel guilty for stepping back, but also relieved because I don’t know if I could handle it on top of everything else.\n\nSo yes, day to day I’m functioning, but it’s taking more effort, and I’ve had to pull back from some things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely not just one thing. If it were only one problem, maybe it would feel easier to pin down, but it’s more like my mind keeps hopping around.\n\nI’ll start out thinking about money — like what bills are coming up, whether we’re stretched too thin — and then before I’ve even settled that in my head, I’m thinking about the relative staying with us and how that affects the house. Then I start worrying about my wife being stressed and whether I’m doing enough or making it worse somehow. It feels like there’s a whole list, and my brain just keeps moving down it, even when I’m trying to focus on whatever’s actually in front of me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

If it were only one problem, maybe it would feel easier to pin down, but it’s more like my mind keeps hopping around.\n\nI’ll start out thinking about money — like what bills are coming up, whether we’re stretched too thin — and then before I’ve even settled that in my head, I’m thinking about the relative staying with us and how that affects the house. Then I start worrying about my wife being stressed and whether I’m doing enough or making it worse somehow. And then it’ll jump to church responsibilities, like something I said I’d help with, or whether I forgot to follow through.\n\nAnd then work gets mixed in too. I’ll be thinking, “Did I mess up? Did I count something wrong? Am I going to make another mistake?” So it’s not really one specific problem. It feels like there’s a whole list, and my brain just keeps moving down it, even when I’m trying to focus on whatever’s actually in front of me."
  }
]
